### 1. Why property is the harder right

The chapter opens by contrasting property with liberty. In the case of liberty **it is easier to detect where an individual's right is likely to come in the way of another's enjoyment of an equal right, so it is easier to determine its proper limits. This is not so easy in the case of the right to property.**

The reason is that property has two origins and neither tracks desert. It is either **something not made by man but occupied or inherited by somebody by chance, by force, or by tradition, such as landed property**, or **a product of the cooperative effort of a large number of individuals, employing different talents and different amounts of labour**. In the second case **their share in the product of their labour is usually not determined by the value of their contribution**, depending instead on **the market forces of demand, supply and competition**, on **the conditions of previous investment due to a pre-existing ownership of property**, and on **level of technological development ... and consequent social relations, such as those between master and slave, lord and serf, capitalist and worker**. Hence it is **an area where there is abundant scope for exploitation**.

The consequence for justification is stated directly: **the prevailing pattern of ownership of property does not reflect an individual's contribution to the social good, or service rendered to society**, and if that continues **it will not be consistent with the moral basis of granting rights to individuals in society**. So the chapter sets its own question: **how can the right to property be made to serve the cause of social justice instead of allowing it to remain a weapon of exploitation and injustice?**

It also names the social stake. The right is called in question **particularly when it leads to concentration of wealth in fewer hands**, since **it is this right that tends to divide society into 'haves' and 'have-nots', and poses a great threat to the spirit of fraternity**. ⚠️ And it rejects one standard consolation outright: disparities **sought to be perpetuated by pseudo-religious pretensions** — poverty read as the outcome of a previous birth or as **a situation divinely ordained** — are, on **modern consciousness**, **a device to cover up the existing injustice in society**, so the justification of property **should ... be sought within the existing institutional framework, without any reference to the prospect of divine compensation**.

### 2. Scope, and the one exclusion that is absolute

**Property** or **private property** denotes **possession of material things which are open to personal or selfish use**, and material things **may include documents, such as promissory notes, patents, shares, guarantees** which **embody an individual's entitlement to property even though such property may not be in his possession for the time being**. Birds and animals may be property.

❌ The exclusion is categorical: **property in the form of human beings, that is the right to keep slaves, is not recognized as legitimate property by modern consciousness although it was so recognized in the past**, because that consciousness **regards all human beings, irrespective of their birth and station, always as end-in-themselves, not as means to an end**, so **no human being, however humble, can be reduced to being the private property of another**.

🔶 That clause is more useful than it looks. It is the only point in the module where a limit on property is derived from the status of persons rather than from distributive consequences, and it is the natural bridge to Lesson 2's slavery reductio and Lesson 12's test that property must not become **an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others**.

### 3. The liberal turn: from an absolute right to an ethical inquiry

Early liberal theory glorified the right. **Machiavelli** observed that **men sooner forget the death of their father than the loss of their patrimony**; **Hobbes** postulated that **the state was created for the security of man's property**; **Locke** held **life, liberty and property** to be **a fundamental natural right**; **Rousseau** described **the security of property as an essential element of civil liberty**; and **Adam Smith**, **Bentham** and other classical liberals treated private property as **an essential instrument of social progress**. 📝 Early liberalism went further and treated possession as **a proof of an individual's stake in the state as also of his political wisdom**, **insisting on linking the 'right to vote' with property qualification** — the clearest demonstration in the module that property and political standing were once explicitly fused.

The turn comes with the experience of industrial capitalism. **Modern liberal theory ... does not recognize any sacred or absolute right to private property.** **J.S. Mill**, who **initially made a strong plea for the security of property**, later argued that it **was not absolute or sacrosanct** and advocated **considerable restriction on the rights of inheritance and bequest**; he criticised **private property in land which was the original inheritance of all mankind** and **pleaded for the taxation of rent**, on the ground that **the rapid appreciation of the value of land and its rent without any effort on the part of landlords** warranted diverting part of that increase **to the use of the community**.

**T.H. Green** valued property **as an instrument for exercising moral freedom**, but held that **when an unrestrained right to property creates conditions under which some men take an unduly large share and others are prevented from acquiring requisite property as a means of their 'self-realization'**, the right **becomes a hindrance to the exercise of freedom in society**. **Hobhouse** went further with **the theory of the social origin of property**, defining taxation as **a measure of securing to society the element in wealth which is the product of social effort, that is which does not owe its origin to the efforts of living individuals, especially of the present owners of property**. **Laski** supplied the test that Lesson 12 develops: the right **should be correlated to the function performed by its owner, that is the service rendered to the state, or the contribution made to the common welfare**.

⚠️ So the modern liberal position is not abolition and not absolutism; it is **an inquiry into the ethical basis** of the right, which **in the process, indicates the proper limits of the right to property**. That is the posture to adopt in an answer, because it is defensible against both flanks.

### 4. Ginsberg's taxonomy, and what each family's strongest exhibit proves

🔶 **Morris Ginsberg**, *On Justice in Society* (**1965**), supplies the organising architecture of the whole chapter — **three types of theories of the right to property, though they are not mutually exclusive**: **natural rights theories; theories of modes of its origin (including the labour theory); and theories of the contribution to the public good**. 📝 Both canonical owners teach the three families without naming their author; naming Ginsberg is a cheap and visible gain in any property answer.

**(i) Natural-rights theories** regard property as **a fundamental right independent of particular institutions**. Their difficulty is historical, and Ginsberg states it in the passage that is the strongest single piece of evidence in this lesson: **it is worth noting that neither in Stoic nor Patristic doctrine is private property a natural right. 'By nature all things are held in common', is Stoic doctrine; the Fathers tell us: 'before the Fall' there was 'no mine and thine distinct'. Private property became necessary and is, therefore, justified because man's greed and avarice have to be kept in check.** Even **in the medieval theory of natural law, private property is not a part of that law though it is not contrary thereto**, and only in **modern scholastic writings** is it **conceded as a 'secondary natural right', which should be exercised under the conditions imposed by natural law**.

⚠️ The force of this is precise and should be stated precisely: the two oldest natural-law traditions treat private property as a **remedial institution justified by human defect**, not as an original entitlement. That inverts the usual rhetorical use of "natural right" in property arguments, and it does so from inside the natural-law tradition.

**Blackstone (1723-80)**, in one clause, supplies the internal limit: he **includes property among absolute rights**, but **seems to apply this concept to 'possession'**, so that **when property becomes a subject of inheritance and bequest, it no longer remains a natural right, but comes within the purview of civil rights**. **Locke** grounds natural right differently — in **inward morality, conscience or the rational faculty of man**, so that the right is **only recognized by the state because it is obliged to do so** — but since he looks for its origin **in human labour, mixed with nature**, **his theory is akin to the 'labour theory'**, which is why he reappears in family (ii).

**(ii) Theories of modes of origin** seek the basis **in the modes of its origin or acquisition, e.g. first occupation, inheritance, labour**. **First occupation** is **only of historical or academic interest**, since it **cannot be related to the mode of acquisition of property in the contemporary world**. **Labour** is **highly important**, and **among the liberal thinkers, Locke is the chief exponent**: every man **by nature has a property in his own person: the labour of his body and the work of his hands are properly his**, and **whatsoever then he removes out of nature and mixes with his labour becomes his property, 'at least where there is enough and as good left in common for others'** (*The Second Treatise of Civil Government*, **1690**). 📝 The authors of the **American Revolution (1776)** and the **French Revolution (1789)** defended property **primarily as the 'fruit of labour'**.

Ginsberg's demolition follows, and it is an internal one: **Locke recognizes that in his view a person is only entitled to such products of his labour as he can use and that everything beyond this is 'more than his share and belongs to others' ... But instead of using his theory as a basis for criticizing existing inequalities, he accepts and justifies them** — attributing those inequalities **to the consent of men**, and arguing that **nature provides no principles for regulating inequalities**. The chapter's own summary: **Locke starts with a progressive idea regarding the true basis of property, but ends up with expediency, forgetting logic. Once even minor inequalities are sought to be justified, larger inequalities automatically find their way.**

Two named extensions complete the family. 🔶 **Macpherson**, *The Political Theory of Possessive Individualism* (**1962**), terms Locke's view **a typical expression of the idea of 'possessive individualism'**: man is **the absolute natural proprietor of his own capacities, owing nothing to society for them**, **freedom is identified with domination over things as manifested in ownership or possession thereof**, society is seen as **a lot of free and equal individuals related to each other through their possessions, and not as those held together by reciprocal rights and duties**, **the relation of exchange, or market relation, is recognized as the fundamental relation of society**, and **political society is seen as a rational device for the protection of property where life and liberty are also reduced to their possessions**. 📝 This is Macpherson's **property** argument and must not be merged with his 1973 developmental/extractive argument at Lesson 6.

🔶 **Jeremy Waldron**, quoted from *The Blackwell Encyclopaedia of Political Thought*, edited by **David Miller** (**1987**), names the mechanism by which the proviso is defeated: acquisition **is limited by Locke's insistence that an owner must not let resources spoil uselessly in his possession**, which **would be sufficient to generate a rough equality of possessions, were it not for the fact that the invention of money, a conventionally-sanctioned durable means of exchange, made it possible for men to own and get the benefit of much more land than they themselves could use the produce of**; and **this legitimation of inequality, and the emphasis on private acquisition and enclosure in Locke's theory, have led many to see him as an ideologist of early modern capitalism**.

The **Utilitarians** are recorded as making the same journey: starting from **the greatest happiness of the greatest number, where each individual was to count as one**, they conceded that **inequality has to be tolerated as a necessary evil, on the ground that equalization would tend to discourage effort, decrease saving, and, by lessening efficiency, diminish the total available for distribution**.

And the **socialist completion**, which is the family's most examinable output: **a distinction is drawn between property in the means of production and property in the fruits of labour. It is clear that, if the instruments of production are appropriated by a few, the rest are deprived of the freedom to apply their labour.** From which **a conclusion is drawn which is the very opposite of Locke's individualism, namely that property in the means of production must be held in common, that all must be given access to apply their labour, and that the assignment of apportionment of the results should be a matter of collective regulation.**

⚠️ Notice that the socialist conclusion is reached **through** the labour theory, not against it. That is why the liberal and socialist property arguments cannot be presented as having different premises; they share the labour premise and differ on whether it is carried to its conclusion.

**(iii) Theories of contribution to the public good** hold that the right **should be tested by their contribution to the public good or well-being**, and the chapter calls these **most significant under the conditions of the present-day world**. Its diagnosis is the lesson's closing fact: with the change in the system of production since the industrial revolution and **the consequent concentration of capital in fewer and fewer hands, there has been an increasing dissociation of property and labour**, and **property has, in modern conditions, often become a means of control over others' life and labour**. These theories therefore **seek to make the right to property subservient to social justice rather than maintain it as an instrument of personal gratification**, and find **fuller expression in social-democratic perspective** — Lesson 12.

### 5. Nozick's three principles, placed where they belong

🔶 Nozick's entitlement apparatus is the contemporary heir of families (i) and (ii) and belongs here rather than with the liberty argument of Lesson 5. Three principles: **acquisition** of an original holding, under a proviso of the kind the desert-water case illustrates; **transfer** by voluntary exchange **without force or fraud**; and **rectification** of holdings traceable to unjust acquisition or transfer.

⚠️ Rectification is the under-stated third principle and the reason the apparatus is worth teaching: **even a libertarian defence of property is not an unconditional defence of the *existing* distribution**, because an existing holding is just only if its history satisfies the first two principles, and the theory itself requires correction where it does not. An answer that states this is doing comparison rather than recitation — and it sets up Lesson 12's rival standard, Tawney's function test, as a disagreement about the *criterion* rather than about whether property may be limited at all.

🔶 **Hegel**, one comparison line only: property is the external sphere in which free will first gives itself objective existence, which explains why *some* property matters to agency — a point that sits between Locke's labour title and Marx's structural critique, and that is not a licence for unlimited accumulation or immunity from regulation.

### 6. The single permitted dated anchor, and exactly what it is allowed to show

✅ **NAKSHA urban property records.** Goa's **Directorate of Settlement and Land Records** issued a public notice dated **2 July 2026** for the NAKSHA Margao inquiry, scheduled **16 July – 14 August 2026**, to examine **rights, titles and interests** before the preparation of a **draft Urban Property Card**. The **Department of Land Resources** describes NAKSHA as a **GIS-integrated urban land-record pilot under the Digital India Land Records Modernization Programme**. Sources: `https://dslr.goa.gov.in/docs/Naksha/Public_Notice_02-07-2026_Margao.pdf` and `https://dolr.gov.in/en/about-naksha/`.

⚠️ **What it is used for, and nothing else.** It illustrates three points this lesson has already made conceptually. First, that **property is documented security**: the chapter's own scope paragraph includes documents that **embody an individual's entitlement to property even though such property may not be in his possession for the time being**, and a record of rights is exactly such a document. Second, that **transparent and contestable procedure is itself a liberty question**: a published notice with a stated window for examining rights, titles and interests is the kind of device Lesson 7 named — general rules, publicity, and a channel through which the affected may contest — as distinct from an untracked administrative determination. Third, that **formal digitisation is not substantive equality of access to verification**: the capacity to appear, understand and contest within a notified window is itself distributed, which is the precise gap Lesson 10 described as opportunities **open to everybody but available only to the chosen few**.

❌ **What it must never be used for.** Not as proof of outcomes; not as a claim that mapping confers conclusive title, redistributes assets or reduces inequality; not re-dated, strengthened or transferred to another lesson; and not supplemented with any further live item. ❌ No other programme, judgment, statute, Article or land-reform measure appears anywhere in this edition.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** On any stem about ownership, titling, access or asset inequality, the three families give an analytical structure that needs no statistics: ask which family the claim belongs to, then apply that family's own internal limit — the Stoic/Patristic evidence against natural-rights claims, Ginsberg's demolition and the money argument against labour claims, and the dissociation of property and labour against public-good claims. ⚠️ The adjacency to stems on property rights as a condition of gender equality is served by exactly this apparatus — **formal title against effective control**, and property as power — and ❌ nothing more; gender discrimination as a subject belongs to its own owner.
- **GS-IV.** The exclusion of persons from the scope of property is the module's cleanest statement of the means/end distinction, and it is stated by the chapter as a change in **modern consciousness** rather than as a theory, which makes it usable without attributing it to any school.
- **Essay.** Ginsberg's Stoic and Patristic evidence is a strong opening for any essay on ownership, because it shows the oldest tradition treating private property as a **remedy for human defect** rather than as a natural entitlement — an inversion the reader does not expect.

**Answer framework — the property-justification stem.** Define property as possession, control and an enforceable claim, and note that documents count → name **Ginsberg (1965)** and the three non-exclusive families in one sentence → take the family the stem relies on and run its strongest exhibit **and** its internal limit → add Nozick's rectification to show that even procedural defences are conditional → close on the chapter's own diagnosis, the **dissociation of property and labour**, which sets up the function test. ⚠️ This framework answers both "justify private property" and "is property a natural right" without changing shape.

Revision notes:

- Property is harder to limit than liberty, because holdings arise **by chance, by force, or by tradition** or from **cooperative effort** in which shares are **usually not determined by the value of their contribution** — hence **abundant scope for exploitation**. The chapter's question: how can the right **serve the cause of social justice instead of ... a weapon of exploitation and injustice**?
- Scope includes **documents, such as promissory notes, patents, shares, guarantees**; ❌ excludes persons, since modern consciousness treats human beings **always as end-in-themselves, not as means to an end**. Pseudo-religious justification of disparity is **a device to cover up the existing injustice**; justification must be sought **within the existing institutional framework**.
- The liberal turn: early liberalism tied **the right to vote** to a property qualification; modern liberal theory **does not recognize any sacred or absolute right** and **investigates the ethical basis** of it. **Mill:** restrict **inheritance and bequest**; tax **rent** on unearned appreciation. **Green:** property enables moral freedom but becomes **a hindrance** when some take **an unduly large share**. **Hobhouse:** taxation secures **the element in wealth which is the product of social effort**. **Laski:** the right is **correlated to the function performed**.
- 🔶 **Ginsberg (1965)** supplies the taxonomy: **natural rights**, **modes of origin (including the labour theory)**, **contribution to the public good** — **not mutually exclusive**.
- Family (i)'s strongest counter-evidence: **by nature all things are held in common** (Stoic); **before the Fall there was no mine and thine distinct** (Patristic); private property justified **because man's greed and avarice have to be kept in check**; medieval law says it is **not a part of that law though it is not contrary thereto**; modern scholastic calls it **a secondary natural right**. **Blackstone**, one clause: absolute as to **possession**, but once a subject of **inheritance and bequest** it becomes a **civil** right.
- **Locke (1690):** property in one's own person; what he **mixes with his labour** becomes his, **at least where there is enough and as good left in common for others**. **Ginsberg's demolition:** Locke concedes the surplus is **more than his share and belongs to others**, but **instead of using his theory as a basis for criticizing existing inequalities, he accepts and justifies them**.
- 🔶 **Macpherson (1962):** **possessive individualism** — man as **absolute natural proprietor of his own capacities, owing nothing to society**; freedom **identified with domination over things**; the **market relation** as society's fundamental relation. 📝 Distinct from his 1973 argument. 🔶 **Waldron (in Miller, ed., 1987):** **money, a conventionally-sanctioned durable means of exchange**, defeats the spoilage limit; **this legitimation of inequality ... have led many to see him as an ideologist of early modern capitalism**.
- **Utilitarian concession:** **inequality has to be tolerated as a necessary evil**. **Socialist completion:** **property in the means of production** against **property in the fruits of labour**; **if the instruments of production are appropriated by a few, the rest are deprived of the freedom to apply their labour**.
- Family (iii): the right **tested by ... contribution to the public good**, against the diagnosis of **an increasing dissociation of property and labour**.
- 🔶 **Nozick:** **acquisition**, **transfer**, **rectification** — the third means even a libertarian defence is conditional on the history of the holding. 🔶 **Hegel**, one line: property as the external sphere of free will — explains why *some* property matters to agency, not unlimited accumulation.
- ✅ **NAKSHA anchor**, used once: property as **documented security**, procedure as a liberty question, and digitisation as not yet substantive equality of access to verification. ❌ Never as proof of outcomes or of conclusive title.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem claims that private property is a natural right. Using Ginsberg's taxonomy, show what must be established for that claim to succeed and why the chapter holds that it fails.

**Model answer:** The claim belongs to the first of **Ginsberg's** three families, which regards property as **a fundamental right independent of particular institutions**. To succeed, it must show that the entitlement exists prior to, and independently of, social enactment. Two things defeat it. First, the historical evidence from inside the natural-law tradition: **neither in Stoic nor Patristic doctrine is private property a natural right. 'By nature all things are held in common', is Stoic doctrine; the Fathers tell us: 'before the Fall' there was 'no mine and thine distinct'**, and private property **became necessary and is, therefore, justified because man's greed and avarice have to be kept in check** — a remedial justification, not an original title. Medieval natural law likewise treats it as **not a part of that law though it is not contrary thereto**, and only modern scholastic writing concedes **a secondary natural right**. Second, the internal limit: even **Blackstone**, who places property among absolute rights, applies that to **possession**, so that once property becomes **a subject of inheritance and bequest, it no longer remains a natural right, but comes within the purview of civil rights**. Locke escapes this by grounding the right in **inward morality**, but since he derives its origin from labour, **his theory is akin to the 'labour theory'** and so must be tested in the second family — where Ginsberg shows that he concedes the surplus is **more than his share and belongs to others** and then **accepts and justifies** existing inequalities instead of criticising them. ⚠️ The verdict is not that property is unjustifiable; it is that its justification is institutional and conditional rather than natural and absolute.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the three families as alternatives from which a theorist picks one. Ginsberg expressly says they are **not mutually exclusive**, and Locke sits in two of them — which is why a claim defeated in the natural-rights family can be rebuilt as a labour claim and must be tested again there.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Discuss the significance of the Stoic and Patristic doctrines for the modern defence of private property. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** Their significance is that they invert the usual justification from inside the tradition that supplies it. **Ginsberg (1965)** records that **neither in Stoic nor Patristic doctrine is private property a natural right**: **'By nature all things are held in common', is Stoic doctrine; the Fathers tell us: 'before the Fall' there was 'no mine and thine distinct'**, and private property **became necessary and is, therefore, justified because man's greed and avarice have to be kept in check**. Three consequences follow. The institution is **remedial**, justified by human defect rather than original entitlement. Its justification is therefore **conditional** on performing that remedial function — the opening Lesson 12's function test exploits. And a modern appeal to "natural right" cannot claim this lineage: medieval natural law treats property as **not a part of that law**, and scholastic writing only as **a secondary natural right**. ⚠️ The doctrines refute an absolute title, not property itself.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 5 + 2 + 2 + 1):** 5 for reproducing both doctrines accurately with the remedial justification attached — greed and avarice **kept in check** — since an answer that reports only "early thinkers favoured common ownership" misses the point entirely. 2 for drawing the correct conclusion, that the institution is conditional and institutional rather than natural and absolute, and naming Ginsberg as the source. 2 for the medieval and scholastic steps, which show the gradation rather than a single doctrine. 1 for the closing limit that the doctrines refute an absolute title and not property as such. Deduct 2 for presenting the doctrines as an argument for abolition.

## Lesson 12 — Limiting property, abolishing it, and the three graded closes

The chapter's last movement is one argument in three beats, and teaching them separately destroys it. Beat one regulates property by tying it to function. Beat two says regulation is insufficient and abolishes the relevant kind. Beat three qualifies the abolition, keeps the surviving test, and closes with three graded limits drawn from three different moral registers.

### 1. Social origin: the argument that removes the self-made premise

The social-democratic perspective **seeks to maintain the right to property on such conditions that serve the purpose of socialism as well as democracy** — the socialist principle requiring social ownership and control of the means of social production, the democratic principle requiring **the security of individual's property as an essential ingredient of his freedom**. The chapter concedes the difficulty — **synthesis of these two principles is difficult to achieve** — and names the route: **to allow individual ownership of property under such regulations that it is largely used to serve social purpose**. Its representatives are **Hobhouse (1864-1929)**, **Tawney (1880-1962)** and **Laski (1893-1950)**.

**Hobhouse**, *Liberalism* (**1911**), begins by relocating the source of a property right. **It is the organized force of society that maintains the rights of owners by protecting them against thieves and depredators**, and those who believe in an absolute right **forget that without the organized force of society their rights are not worth a week's purchase**. The illustration is the passage worth carrying: **the prosperous businessman who thinks that he has made his fortune entirely by self help does not pause to consider what single step he could have taken on the road to his success but for the ordered tranquility which has made commercial development possible, the security by road, and rail, and sea, the masses of skilled labour, and the sum of intelligence which civilization has placed at his disposal.**

The argument is then pressed beyond security: even **the demand for the goods which a businessman supplies is created by the general progress of the world**, and modern production rests on **inventions which have been built up by the collective effort of generations of men of science and organizers of industry**. So **it is society which maintains and guarantees his possessions**, and **society which is an indispensable partner in its original creation**.

⚠️ This is the premise on which taxation ceases to be a taking. Lesson 11 already carried Hobhouse's definition of taxation as **a measure of securing to society the element in wealth which is the product of social effort**; the social-origin argument is what makes that definition more than an assertion.

### 2. From origin to power: the two printed qualifications

In *The Elements of Social Justice* (**1922**) Hobhouse moves from where property comes from to what it does. **We find many forms of property which involve power over other persons ... The owner of land may be in a position to determine the means by which many people can earn their living ... If the property is important and limited, ownership may involve a partial or complete monopoly, carrying with it a considerable, perhaps a fundamental, power over the lives of many ... If, again, there is no monopoly, but wealth is very unevenly distributed, then the possessors of capital have advantages in contracting with workers which give them a very large measure of control over labour.**

The chapter adds that this control **is more keenly felt in modern industry**, with its **concentration of control, unified direction, immense increase in labour force**, enabling the employer **to dictate his terms to the mass of workers** and to **make huge profits which tend to widen the disparity between the haves and have-nots**, while the profit motive also leads him to **disregard public needs** and avoid **such supplies and services which are less profitable but greatly needed by the community**.

Hence the **two main qualifications** on the right, stated as printed: **(a) it is not used as an instrument of power over others; and (b) it is exercised with due regard to the needs of the community.**

🔶 Note that (a) is a domination claim in the vocabulary of Lesson 7 — the objection is to **power over other persons**, not merely to a larger share — and that the last clause of the quoted passage locates that power in **advantages in contracting with workers**, which is Lesson 2's bargaining-power problem again. Three lessons converge on one sentence, and saying so in an answer is what distinguishes synthesis from recall.

### 3. Tawney's function test

**Tawney**, *The Acquisitive Society* (**1920**), supplies the formulation that wins marks on sight. He deprecates **the tyranny of functionless property** and advocates **subordination of property to social service**: **functionless property appears natural to those who believe that society should be organized for the acquisition of private wealth ... Those, however, who hold that social unity and effective work are possible only if society is organized and wealth distributed on the basis of function, will ask of an institution not, 'What dividends does it pay?' but 'What service does it perform?'**

⚠️ Use the test as a **question**, not as a slogan. Its analytic value is that it is neutral between institutions — it can be put to a firm, a landholding, an office or a programme — and it converts a distributive complaint into an inquiry about the holding's role, which is harder to dismiss.

### 4. Laski's complete scheme, compressed but not thinned

**Laski**, *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**), gives the perspective its full form. ⚠️ The long passages are paraphrased here with their titles and years attached; the short decisive formulations are reproduced.

**The moral basis.** **If property must be possessed in order that a man may be his best self, the existence of such a right is clear ... I have the right to property if what I own is, broadly speaking, important for the service I perform. I have the right to own if what I own can be shown to be related to the common welfare as a condition of its maintenance.** And negatively: **no man ... has a moral right to property except as a return for function performed ... Those whose property is the result of other men's effort are parasitic upon society. They enjoy what they have not assisted to produce.**

📝 Laski is **not** hostile to the institution. He concedes that possession supplies **a means of harmony in the shape of security**, that **the man of property has a stake in the country** and gains **opportunity to develop creative tastes for art and culture**, and that **poverty means a miserable life which destroys man's creative impulse**, so that **the institution of private property cannot be condemned as bad in itself**. His objection is to the observed fact that **any significant property is owned by a small number, and its ownership is not necessarily related to the performance of duties or possession of virtues**. The capitalist diagnosis is structural: ownership **involves the control of capital, and ... the control of capital involves power to direct the lives of those who depend upon the application of capital to production**, so that **a regime of private property makes the State very largely an institution dominated by the owners of private property**.

**The five defences, demolished.** The **psychological** defence makes property an incentive to labour; Laski replies that owners **work for private profit, not for the good of the community**, and amass property which **inhibits their descendants from working at all**, so **property may defeat more incentives than it creates**. The **ethical** defence treats property as a return for effort; but **the amount of reward depends on the capacity to make profit, i.e. on manipulation, not on the value of service rendered to society**. The **nurse-of-virtues** defence credits property with fostering love of family, generosity, inventiveness and energy; but **these virtues have been present in those persons also who have never amassed property at all**. The **effective-demand** defence says property is the reward for supplying what is demanded; Laski answers with a reductio: **there is a demand for slaves in Abyssinia; but most men will, I think, agree that response to the demand ought never to be allowed. There is a demand for obscene literature; but few would respect those who trafficked in it. There is a demand for prostitutes; but the law has a definite answer to those who live by satisfying it.** The **historical** defence holds that progressive societies rest on private property; Laski concedes **some truth in it** but shows that the conception has changed — **property in slaves was valid in Greece and Rome; it is no longer valid today. In England there is great freedom of testamentary disposition; in France inheritance is regulated with much stringency** — so **no pervasive right to property can be established on historical grounds**. He adds a direct conclusion on **limitation of the right of inheritance, on moral grounds**.

⚠️ The effective-demand reductio is the most portable item in this block, because it generalises: the existence of a demand cannot by itself justify the reward for meeting it, which is a point usable far beyond property.

**The theory of reward.** Laski rejects three candidates. **Equal rewards** fail because **there is no justice in an equal reward for unequal effort**, and because **needs are also unequal**. **Market-determined reward** fails because the market **is largely manipulated by experts in advertising and salesmanship who distort social need to create a demand for their own products**, so that **the prevailing price system is seldom related to the value of the service performed**. **From each according to his ability, to each according to his needs** fails as **an over-simplification of a complex issue**, since it supplies no answer to **who will determine the ability** and **who will determine the genuine needs of each individual**, and is therefore **hardly capable of practical application**.

What survives is a two-condition test: any principle of reward must **enable the individual to reach out towards his best self** and must **preserve and develop necessary functions of society**. Plus provision for **children, old people, disabled and defective persons, who cannot pay their way**. Which yields the **common civic minimum**, **beyond which the reward will increase with the value of the service rendered to society**.

**The theory of industrial organization.** **Industry, in fact, must be made a profession. It needs to be informed by a principle of public service. It must not be merely a body of persons who are turning out goods for profit.** Four structural changes follow: the owner of capital **should receive a fixed dividend for the service rendered by the loan of his capital**, no longer profiting **by the special ability of management, the rise in price or the special privilege of a monopoly**; **rules for the industry will be laid by its working force**, since **once the functionless owner of capital is removed, an industry becomes an intelligible entity**; essentials **necessary for the well-being of the community, but hardly fetch private profit** are socialised; and all industries are regulated by **standard hours and standard rates of pay**, with recruitment on **proven competence, as required in the case of entry into the bar or medical practice**.

**The size test**, which is the best single closing line for any property answer: **there is nothing inherently wrong in the notion of private prosperity ... It must never be so large in amount that its owner exercises power by reason of its sheer magnitude; it must never be so small that its possessor cannot be himself at his best. The more equal its distribution, the more likely is the contribution of the citizen to be judged in terms of its social value.**

📝 The chapter's own assessment is candid: if Laski's scheme **is seriously put to practice, it will eliminate capitalists**, though **a large number of 'shareholders'** may remain, so that **Laski apparently seeks to maintain capitalism in a subdued form**, hoping **to do away with the exploitative character of capitalism**.

### 5. The Marxist beat: abolish, and be precise about what

The Marxist account refuses the appeal that the previous beat depends on. Bourgeois property **creates its domination over society through the blind laws of market forces**, is **devoid of any human sense or human appeal**, and is **a dehumanizing force: no useful purpose will be served by trying to humanize it** — and in any case the owners **are themselves slaves of their private property no less than the workers**, so moral suasion has no addressee. Hence **the obvious course is the abolition of private property itself**.

The precision is what matters in an answer. **The distinguishing feature of Communism is not the abolition of property generally, but the abolition of bourgeois property** (*Communist Manifesto*, **1848**) — bourgeois private property being **the final and most complete expression of the system of producing and appropriating products, that is based on class antagonisms, on the exploitation of the many by the few**. The reason is given in the same place: **to be a capitalist, is to have not only a purely personal, but a social status in production. Capital is a collective product, and only by the united action of many members ... can it be set in motion. Capital is, therefore, not a personal, it is a social power.** And therefore **communism deprives no man of the power to appropriate the products of society: all that it does is to deprive him of the power to subjugate the labour of others by means of such appropriation.**

What survives abolition is stated explicitly: **personal property — a genuine fruit of an individual's labour and the instrument of his freedom — will remain intact**, with ownership changing **from bourgeois ownership to social ownership, from class ownership to common ownership**, though **in the transitional phase of the 'dictatorship of the proletariat' it may temporarily be held in class ownership of the proletariat**.

⚠️ Two errors to avoid. The first is reporting the position as the abolition of all property; the Manifesto expressly denies it. The second is treating *personal* and *private* as interchangeable; the whole argument turns on the distinction, and so does the chapter's own qualification in the next beat.

🔶 **The equity-versus-equality link, taken only as far as this chapter supports it.** The distributive formula of the transitional stage is **from each according to his ability, to each according to his work**, which Lenin described as **a 'bourgeois right' ... retained in socialist society for practical reasons** because it at least ensures **the right to work** and **the right to maintenance**; the communist stage adopts **from each according to his ability, to each according to his need**. So the contribution standard and the need standard are **stages**, not rivals — which is precisely why a stem about equity against equality in Marxian terms is answered by the sequence and not by a definition. ➡️ Justice as a theory belongs to Topics 19 and 20.

### 6. The qualification, and the test that survives it

The chapter praises the distinction and then limits the conclusion drawn from it. The personal/private distinction **is a laudable idea. However, it raises some difficulties in actual practice.** The Marxist theory **is fit for application to a society which is divided into two clearcut classes — haves and have-nots**, but **the size of middle class has considerably increased and is steadily expanding**, and when **a very large number of shareholders have invested their hard-earned money and savings in big business and industries, then the situation turns out to be more complex**. When a middle-class person **raises his personal property by dint of his talent and effort, and invests his savings in the shares of selected companies for the security of his future, then the dividing line between 'personal property' and 'private property' in the Marxian sense starts blurring**, so that **recourse to a violent revolution for the abolition of 'private property' would lose its rationale**.

⚠️ Note exactly what the qualification does and does not defeat. It defeats the **revolutionary conclusion**; it does not defeat the **test**. The chapter immediately retains the test: **we must appreciate an important suggestion of Marxist theory, that any form of property should not be allowed to become an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others.** And it grades the verdict: **as long as the right to property provides freedom and a sense of security to the individual, and facilitates the best use of his talent and effort, it must be admired. But when this right gives rise to glaring economic disparities in society and forces the bulk of workers to lead a sub-human life, it must be strictly regulated.**

📝 The shareholding observation is **2009-era sociology** and is used here only to support the qualification it was offered for. ❌ It is not a claim about any present ownership pattern, market or class structure.

### 7. The three graded closes

**Aristotle** supplies a limit of degree: **a certain amount of private property was necessary for good life and for exercising virtue, but when acquired beyond this limit it becomes a source of vice**. ⚠️ With the chapter's own boundary attached, which is the detail most answers miss: **here Aristotle was dealing with the impact of property on personal morality, not with its impact on social organization.** So the close is real but narrow.

**Proudhon (1809-65)** supplies the limit of principle, in four words: **property is theft**. 📝 He is introduced as one of the **early socialists** who were **deeply concerned with the institution of property as a source of vast socio-economic disparities and social injustice** and **sought to transform it thoroughly**.

**Gandhi (1869-1948)** supplies the limit that spans both registers, and the chapter is careful about which principle operates where. In **personal morality**, **non-possession (renunciation or aparigraha)** and **non-stealing (abstention from theft or asteya)**: a person **should not acquire any material things beyond his immediate requirement because amassing of wealth is bound to degrade his character**, and it **also amounts to theft because thereby you deprive others of their due share** — summarised in the line that is the module's single most quotable Indian formulation: **the earth has enough to satisfy everybody's need, but it does not have enough to satisfy anybody's greed**. Spanning **personal as well as social morality**, **bread labour**: everybody **should do physical labour, apart from his usual duties, and contribute to the production of material things in order to compensate for his consumption**, so that scarcity **will not be further aggravated**, and which is also commended **as an instrument of self-purification**. In **social morality**, **trusteeship**: it **requires the owners of capital and big estate to treat their possessions as the trust of humanity and employ them for social service, not for personal gratification, not to speak of private profit**. The chapter's conclusion: if these principles are adopted, **tyranny of property will automatically disappear and there will be a general uplift of material and moral life of society**.

❌ **One block only.** Gandhian philosophy as a system — satyagraha, swaraj, the critique of modern civilisation, the constructive programme — belongs to **Topic 05**, and nothing beyond the printed property close is taught here.

### 8. The topic's verdict

Three findings, and every 15- and 20-mark model in the final arc defends them. **Liberty becomes just only as equal liberty**, since freedom built on the unfreedom of others **means the rule of limited numbers**. **Equality becomes real only when it reaches the socio-economic sphere**, since legal and political equality **may be rendered ineffective due to socio-economic inequality**. And **property is defensible only while it stays function-linked and justly held** — tested by **what service does it perform?**, by the two Hobhousian qualifications, by Laski's size test, and by the surviving Marxist condition that it must not become **an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others**.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

- **GS-II.** On any stem about ownership, concentration, or the social obligations attaching to wealth, the chain social origin → power over persons → function test gives a complete analytical spine with four named authorities and no statistics. ⚠️ The conceptual adjacency to stems on inequality in resource ownership is exactly Hobhouse's **power over other persons** clause; all data and policy content belongs to `Economy/` and `Social-Justice/`.
- **GS-IV.** Trusteeship and Tawney's function test are the two cleanest statements in the module of a duty attaching to a holding rather than to a transaction, and Laski's **parasitic upon society** supplies the negative formulation. Gandhi's need/greed line is usable in almost any ethics answer about consumption or public resources.
- **Essay.** The *Acquisitive Society* question — not **what dividends does it pay?** but **what service does it perform?** — is among the most efficient essay hinges available, because it can be asked of any institution the essay names.

**Answer framework — 20 marks on property.** Define property and name **Ginsberg's** three families in one clause (Lesson 11) → state the social-origin premise, which removes the self-made assumption → apply the function test as the criterion → run one objection–reply pair: Tawney's function test against **Nozick's rectification** as a rival standard, since both limit the existing distribution but by different tests → state the Marxist abolition argument **precisely**, including the personal/private distinction → give the chapter's qualification and the surviving test → close with one graded limit, preferably Gandhi's trusteeship, and the verdict that property is defensible while function-linked and justly held.

Revision notes:

- The synthesis sought: individual ownership **under such regulations that it is largely used to serve social purpose**; representatives **Hobhouse**, **Tawney**, **Laski**. **Hobhouse (1911), social origin:** **it is the organized force of society that maintains the rights of owners**; without it **their rights are not worth a week's purchase**; the **prosperous businessman** passage; society is **an indispensable partner in its original creation**.
- **Hobhouse (1922), power:** **many forms of property which involve power over other persons**; monopoly gives **a fundamental power over the lives of many**; uneven wealth gives **advantages in contracting with workers** and **a very large measure of control over labour**. **Two qualifications:** property must not be **used as an instrument of power over others**, and must be **exercised with due regard to the needs of the community**.
- **Tawney (1920):** against **functionless property** — ask **not, 'What dividends does it pay?' but 'What service does it perform?'**
- **Laski (1938), moral basis:** the right exists **if what I own is ... important for the service I perform**; **no man ... has a moral right to property except as a return for function performed**; owners of others' effort are **parasitic upon society**. 📝 Laski's concession: property gives **security**, **a stake in the country** and scope for **creative tastes**; **poverty ... destroys man's creative impulse**; the institution **cannot be condemned as bad in itself**.
- **Five defences demolished:** incentive (**property may defeat more incentives than it creates**); reward-for-effort (reward turns on **manipulation**, not **the value of service**); nurse of virtues (present in those who **never amassed property at all**); **effective demand** (slaves in Abyssinia, obscene literature, prostitution); historical (slaves valid in **Greece and Rome**; **England** against **France** on testamentary freedom) — property is **a changing conception**. Plus limitation of **inheritance ... on moral grounds**.
- **Theory of reward:** equal rewards unjust (**no justice in an equal reward for unequal effort**); market reward distorted by **experts in advertising and salesmanship who distort social need**; need-based distribution **hardly capable of practical application**. Two conditions — reach **his best self**, and **preserve and develop necessary functions of society** — plus provision for those who **cannot pay their way**, resolving into a **common civic minimum**.
- **Theory of industrial organization:** **industry ... must be made a profession**, **informed by a principle of public service**; owner reduced to a **fixed dividend**; rules by **the working force** once **the functionless owner of capital is removed**; socialised essentials; **standard hours and standard rates**; recruitment on **proven competence**. **The size test:** never **so large ... that its owner exercises power by reason of its sheer magnitude**; never **so small that its possessor cannot be himself at his best**.
- **Marxist abolition:** bourgeois property rules through **the blind laws of market forces**; owners are **slaves of their private property no less than the workers**; the distinguishing feature is **not the abolition of property generally, but the abolition of bourgeois property**; **capital is ... not a personal, it is a social power**; communism **deprives no man of the power to appropriate the products of society**, only of **the power to subjugate the labour of others**; **personal property ... will remain intact**.
- 🔶 Stages, not rivals: **to each according to his work** in the socialist stage — Lenin's **bourgeois right** retained for practical reasons, securing **the right to work** and **the right to maintenance** — and **to each according to his need** in the communist stage.
- **The qualification:** the theory fits **two clearcut classes**, but an expanding **middle class** and mass **shareholders** blur **the dividing line**, so **violent revolution ... would lose its rationale**. 📝 2009-era sociology, used only for this purpose.
- **The surviving test:** **any form of property should not be allowed to become an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others**; property is **admired** while it gives freedom, security and best use of talent, and **strictly regulated** when it produces **glaring economic disparities** and a **sub-human life** for workers.
- **Three closes:** **Aristotle** — property necessary **for good life and for exercising virtue**, beyond the limit **a source of vice**, ⚠️ concerning **personal morality, not ... social organization**; **Proudhon** — **property is theft**; **Gandhi** — **aparigraha** and **asteya** (personal), **bread labour** (both, also **self-purification**), **trusteeship** (social), with **the earth has enough to satisfy everybody's need, but ... not ... anybody's greed**. ❌ One Gandhi block only; Gandhian philosophy is Topic 05's.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the distinction between personal and private property in the Marxist account, and state the chapter's qualification of it without abandoning the test that survives.

**Model answer:** The distinction is between property as the fruit of one's own labour and property as a position in production. **Private property**, in the relevant sense, denotes the means of production, because **it is the mode of ownership of the means of production which determines how the 'have-nots' will earn their livelihood**. **Personal property** is **a genuine fruit of an individual's labour and the instrument of his freedom** and **will remain intact**. The *Communist Manifesto* is explicit that **the distinguishing feature of Communism is not the abolition of property generally, but the abolition of bourgeois property**, and gives the reason: **capital is a collective product** and therefore **not a personal, it is a social power**, so communism **deprives no man of the power to appropriate the products of society: all that it does is to deprive him of the power to subjugate the labour of others by means of such appropriation**. The chapter calls this **a laudable idea** and then qualifies it: the analysis fits a society of **two clearcut classes**, but an expanding **middle class** and a very large number of **shareholders** investing savings mean **the dividing line ... starts blurring**, so **recourse to a violent revolution ... would lose its rationale**. ⚠️ What the qualification defeats is the revolutionary conclusion, not the criterion. The chapter retains the criterion in the next sentence: **any form of property should not be allowed to become an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others** — which is the same test Hobhouse states as power over other persons and Tawney as **what service does it perform?**

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not write that Marxism abolishes all property, and do not use *personal* and *private* interchangeably. The whole argument — and the whole force of the chapter's qualification — depends on the distinction, and an answer that loses it cannot state either the position or the objection.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "No man has a moral right to property except as a return for function performed." Comment. *(Comment — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** The proposition is Laski's (*A Grammar of Politics*, **1938**) and holds as a criterion, not as a description. Its ground is Hobhouse's social-origin argument: **it is the organized force of society that maintains the rights of owners**, and without it **their rights are not worth a week's purchase**, so an owner's claim presupposes a social contribution he did not make. Its test is Tawney's: **not, 'What dividends does it pay?' but 'What service does it perform?'** Its negative form follows — those whose property is the result of others' effort are **parasitic upon society**. ✅ Laski's own concession counts: property supplies **security**, **a stake in the country** and scope for creative life, so the institution **cannot be condemned as bad in itself**. ⚠️ Comment accordingly: it is a sound standard for *justifying* holdings, and its own author grades it — a **common civic minimum**, then reward rising **with the value of the service rendered**.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 2 + 3 + 2 + 3):** 2 for grounding the proposition rather than restating it, using Hobhouse's social-origin argument to show why an owner's claim is not self-standing. 3 for operationalising it with Tawney's function question and Laski's negative formulation, so that the answer supplies a *test* and not a sentiment. 2 for including Laski's own concession that property has genuine value, since a **Comment** directive requires the proposition to be weighed rather than endorsed. 3 for the graded close — civic minimum, then reward scaled to service — which shows that the author himself does not treat the proposition as all-or-nothing. Deduct 2 for presenting the line as an argument for abolishing property, and deduct 2 for naming any land, tenancy or ceiling law.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## Ownership

Political Theory is **supplementary conceptual support**. The subject's own syllabus mapping records that this folder is **not a direct named owner of any General Studies or Optional question**, that the count of question routes into its canonical files is **zero by design**, and that no proxy, alias, mirror ledger or synthetic assignment may be created to produce a non-zero count. ⚠️ That zero is a consequence of the conceptual-support boundary, not a gap in coverage.

**Nothing in this edition is presented as a Political Theory previous-year question, because none exists.** Every stem named below is a **deployment target owned by another subject**, named at every use. No answer approach is offered on its owner's behalf beyond identifying which lessons supply the apparatus, and every solved item in the practice section is original work written for this edition.

⚠️ **This is the densest Optional cluster in the module, and that density is a trap.** The Philosophy socio-political owners hold these questions; this edition supplies only the concepts. An Optional candidate must write from those owners, using this file for the argument spine and the exact formulations.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| **Decisive** | Critically evaluate the concepts of **liberty and equality as political ideals** | 15 | Philosophy — Social and Political Ideals | **L10**, L1 (what a restraint must be), L8 (right not fact), L5 (the strongest objection) | As above |
| **Decisive** | How far can **liberty and equality** be considered **distinctive features of democracy**? | 20 | Philosophy — Social and Political Ideals | **L10** (legal-political reconciliation; political equality as **one man, one vote** with **no privileged classes ... entitled to rule**), L2 (the media-ownership caveat), L6 | Democratic theory and institutional design, which are Topics 22 and 23 |
| **Decisive** | **"How are both equality and liberty inadequate as social and political ideals without justice?"** Discuss | 20 | Philosophy — Social and Political Ideals | **L10** (Aristotle; libertarianism against egalitarianism; Hobhouse's close), L12 (the function test as the surviving standard) | ❌ **Theories of justice** — the original position, the veil, the two principles, the difference principle — which belong to **Topics 19/20** |

The original position, the veil, the two principles as a system and the difference principle belong to **Topics 19/20** |

⚠️ **Two routing cautions.** First, the four decisive liberty-and-equality stems are **not four questions**; they are one question asked at four lengths, and the sphere verdict plus the settlement line answers all four, with the thinker-density and the objection–reply count scaled to marks. Second, the property-and-gender cluster is the one place where a candidate is most likely to over-reach: this edition supplies **property as power** and **formal title against effective control**, and stops. The substantive gender argument is another file's.

## Directive decoder for the stems above

| Directive | What it actually demands | The move that earns the marks here |
|---|---|---|
| **Discuss** | Present the competing positions and arrive at a position | Name the two camps across the socio-economic band, then adjudicate with the sphere test |
| **Critically evaluate / critically discuss** | State the strongest version of the view, then test it | Run one full objection–reply pair; Hayek's mirage against the chapter's reply is the default |
| **Examine** | Inspect the claim's structure and conditions | Separate the conditional from the conclusion, as in Hayek's antagonism argument |
| **Comment** | Weigh a proposition rather than endorse it | Concede the half that is true before narrowing it — Laski's own concession on property is the model |
| **How far** | Supply a graded, sphere-qualified answer | "Reconciled in the legal-political sphere; contested in the socio-economic sphere" is the grading |
| **Distinguish / difference between** | Supply differentia, not two descriptions | Use the axis structure: source, test, remedy |

⚠️ Most of the relevant Philosophy set are **propositions**, not questions. For those, run the five-move micro-method: identify the proposition and the concept it presupposes → reconstruct its strongest argument as premises, mechanism and conclusion → test the counterargument and state the conditions under which the proposition would hold → apply named evidence from the lessons → give a graded verdict of *holds / holds only under stated conditions / fails as stated*.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

Four checks: three consolidate a block, and the fourth spans the whole arc. Each model is held within 250 words.

### Cumulative check A — Lessons 1 to 4

**Question:** Build the chain from the definition of liberty to the alterability test, naming each step and the clause that carries it.

**Model answer:** One: liberty is formally **absence of restraint**, but **'unrestrained' does not mean 'unregulated'** — only **unreasonable restraints** are barred, on the **Legitimacy** standard, since the arrangement must be regarded as **lawful by those affected by it**. Two: the frontier is double — unlimited liberty becomes **licence**, in which **one man's liberty would become another's constraint or oppression**, while unlimited authority yields **unconditional submission ... hence the loss of liberty**; Barker balances them with the owner's **relative liberty which must be adjusted to the liberty of the worker** to remain **a free agent**. Three: the test operates in three capacities, and the third is decisive: **this is the sphere where the adjustment between the claims of liberty and equality becomes most essential**, because without **such a substantial equality between the parties**, **one party acts under a degree of compulsion**. Four: Raphael classifies the aspects by **freedom to do what?** and **freedom from what?**, and the thesis is that they **are complementary; they are two sides of the same coin**, differing only in the **role of the state**. Five: Berlin restricts political liberty to the negative sphere, holding that **the availability or non-availability of means is entirely an individual's own concern**; the chapter replies that he **confused the two spheres ... the moral sphere and the material sphere**, since socially produced deprivation is **coercion by other individuals** and qualifies **by Berlin's own standards**. ⚠️ The resulting gate licenses a political claim where the impediment is socially produced and removable, and withholds it where it is not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the alterability gate as a test of justice. It settles jurisdiction — whether a complaint belongs in politics — and Lesson 9's relevant-ground test is what settles whether correction is owed.

### Cumulative check B — Lessons 5 to 7

**Question:** Show how the libertarian, Marxist and republican accounts each relocate the source of unfreedom, and what each cannot absorb.

**Model answer:** The **libertarian** locates it in the **arbitrary will of another**: Hayek's man is free **when he is not subject to coercion by the arbitrary will of another**, and Friedman's freedom is **the absence of coercion of a man by his fellow men**, with **competitive capitalism** as **a necessary though not a sufficient condition**; political freedom, inner freedom and **freedom as power** are excluded as different things. It cannot absorb the market as a coercive relation — Macpherson's **elementary conceptual error**, that in a capitalist economy **labour force exists without its own sufficient capital, hence it has no choice in the market** — nor can it stop Hayek's cake argument from reducing the individual from **an end** to **a means**. The **Marxist** account relocates unfreedom into the structure of production: **the members of civil society are not atoms**, freedom requires **the mastery and rational control of the process of production**, and alienation runs from product to process to fellow men to self, with Marcuse adding that manufactured wants ensure **the genuine urge for freedom disappears**. It cannot absorb the problem of who judges authentic need — **human needs are endless** — and its own author's turn to **the marginal elite of unbrainwashed students** costs him his Marxist standing. The **republican** relocates it in a **relationship**: domination is **capacity** plus **arbitrariness** plus **impunity**, so freedom is a status and the benevolent master's slave is unfree though un-interfered-with. ⚠️ It cannot escape **overbreadth** without a threshold of "effectively constrained", which the theory does not fix.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present these as three degrees of the same complaint. They disagree about *where* unfreedom lives — in another's will, in the structure of production, or in the standing between two parties — and that is why their remedies differ in kind rather than in intensity.

### Cumulative check C — Lessons 8 to 10

**Question:** Move from equality as a statement of right to the verdict on where it collides with liberty, marking what each step adds.

**Model answer:** One: **equality is a prescriptive term, not a descriptive one. We argue that men must be treated as equal, not that they are in fact equal** — so the argument from natural difference misses its target. Two: Rousseau divides the field — **natural or physical inequality** of **age, health, bodily strength and qualities of mind and soul** against **conventional** inequality of **wealth, prestige and power**, the latter **largely man-made** and the only political target; the 1789 Declaration concludes that **social distinctions can be based only upon public utility**. Three: Tawney's formula — treat people **in the same way insofar as, being human, they have requirements which are the same, and in different ways insofar as ... they have requirements which differ** — two operations, not one. Four: the permitted grounds are **need** and **excellence**, fixed by **the level of social consciousness of a given society**, excellence being conditioned by the proviso that it be **beneficial to society**, not by the **market society model**. Five: equality climbs three rungs — legal, political, socio-economic, the last **articulated as a goal of socialism** — and the collision is located, not general: **in the legal-political sphere, a reconciliation ... is not at all difficult. But their relation in the socio-economic sphere is certainly more complicated**, and **even legal and political equality may be rendered ineffective due to socio-economic inequality**. ⚠️ Six: the settlement is a concession, not a victory — **equality of opportunity, not identical treatment; fair shares, not equal shares** — bounded by the warning that stretching differential reward **too far** makes it **untenable**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report the conflict as general. The whole analytical yield of this block is the asymmetry between the two bands, and an answer that writes "liberty and equality are in tension" without locating the tension has discarded it.

### Cumulative check D — the whole arc, Lessons 1 to 12

**Question:** In one connected argument, move from the definition of liberty to a graded verdict on property, marking where each lesson does work.

**Model answer:** Liberty bars **unreasonable restraints** only, judged by those affected, and sits between licence and authority (L1). It operates in three capacities, the economic one deciding, since **this is the sphere where the adjustment between the claims of liberty and equality becomes most essential** (L2). Its negative and positive aspects are **two sides of the same coin**, differing on the state's role (L3). Berlin confines political liberty to the negative sphere; the reply is that he merged the **moral** and **material** spheres, so socially produced deprivation counts **by Berlin's own standards**, and the alterability gate follows (L4). The libertarians deny the move — Hayek's three rejected meanings, Friedman's **necessary though not a sufficient condition**, Nozick's minimal state — each failing on its own premise (L5). The Marxist account relocates unfreedom into production, with alienation, manufactured wants and **developmental** against **extractive** power (L6). The republican reframes it as a status, so regulating a dominating party **produces** liberty rather than trading it (L7). Equality is then **a statement of right, not of fact**, dividing natural from conventional inequality (L8), permitting difference only on **need** and socially **beneficial excellence** (L9), and colliding with liberty only in the socio-economic band, settled by **fair shares, not equal shares** (L10). Property is the asset on which all this bites: Ginsberg's three families each fail their own strongest test (L11), so the right survives only as **a return for function performed**, never **an instrument of exploitation and control over lives of others** (L12). ⚠️ Verdict: equal liberty, substantive equality, function-linked property.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not let the chain become a tour of thinkers. Each step is load-bearing: remove the reasonable-restraint test and licence cannot be distinguished from freedom; remove the alterability gate and every disadvantage becomes a political claim; remove the sphere verdict and the conflict becomes general; remove the function test and property has no criterion at all.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Original practice prompts written for this edition. They are **not** previous-year questions and must not be cited as such. 10-mark models are held within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models within 250.

**Practice question 1 [10]:** Distinguish liberty from licence, and state the test that separates them. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Licence is defined by totality, liberty by reasonableness. Licence treats liberty as **the absence or removal of all restraints on the actions of individual in utter disregard of the interest of other individuals**; liberty bars only **unreasonable restraints**, since **'unrestrained' does not mean 'unregulated'**. Three differentia follow. *Effect*: licence is self-defeating, because **one man's liberty would become another's constraint or oppression** and **the large fish will be free to swallow the smaller one**. *Structure*: liberty is relational — Barker's factory owner holds **a relative liberty which must be adjusted to the liberty of the worker** to remain **a free agent**, so that liberty **would not exist unless it were regulated**. *Test*: the standard is not the restrainer's but the **Legitimacy** standard of those affected, who must find the arrangement **reasonable and beneficial**. ⚠️ Hobhouse states the general form: **a freedom to be enjoyed by all must impose some restraint upon all**.

**Scoring note:** a **Distinguish** directive is answered by differentia, not by two definitions. The three axes — effect, structure, test — supply them, and the Legitimacy clause closes the obvious follow-up about who decides reasonableness.

**Practice question 2 [10]:** Explain why formal equality before the law may not produce equal justice. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Because access is resource-dependent. Legal equality means **equal legal status to all individuals ... irrespective of their birth, physical and mental capacities**, and Lucas states its precise content: equality before the law **does not guarantee equal treatment by the law but equal access to the law**, since **nobody is so lowly as not to have recourse to the courts**. The chapter then records the mechanism of failure: it is **no guarantee of justice in a society characterized by extremes of wealth and poverty**, because **legal costs remain exorbitant** and **legal procedures remain too intricate**; Laski adds that **the ablest lawyers will be at the service of those only who are able to afford them**. Benn and Peters generalise it — **without social equality, 'equality before the law' would remain an empty form** — and opportunities become **a mere piece of decoration, open to everybody but available only to the chosen few**.

**Scoring note:** an **Explain** directive requires the mechanism, not the complaint. The answer supplies it — an access right whose use is resource-dependent — and avoids any constitutional or case material, which belongs to another subject.

**Practice question 3 [15]:** "Equality does not mean identity of treatment." Critically examine, with reference to the grounds on which unequal treatment may be justified. *(Critically examine — answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The criterion first: does the proposition empty equality or specify it? It specifies it, which is why it holds. Laski gives the reason: **there can be no ultimate identity of treatment so long as men are different in want and capacity and need. The purpose of society would be frustrated at the outset if the nature of a mathematician met an identical response with that to the nature of a bricklayer.** Tawney's formula makes it operable, in **two** operations — treat people **in the same way insofar as, being human, they have requirements which are the same, and in different ways insofar as ... they have requirements which differ**. The permitted grounds are fixed, not open: **need**, applied through progressive taxation sparing those **already living at subsistence level**, services distributed **not according to the amount of taxes that they pay**, and **merit-cum-means** scholarships where **means would be the first consideration**; and **excellence**, entering only **when basic 'needs' have been largely met** and only where **beneficial to society**, which disqualifies the **market society model** that lets **manipulators** profit **at the expense of the vulnerable sections**. The examination must be two-sided. The proposition can be stretched: those who say the state **has nothing to do with the existing socio-economic inequalities** are **stretching the principle of liberty too far**. And the reverse-discrimination objections are real: preference attaching to groups **rather than individually as deprived persons** invites **intra-group capture**. ⚠️ Verdict: correct, and it requires a stated ground for every departure from sameness.

**Scoring note:** the directive demands a criterion, two grounds and a limit. The answer supplies the Tawney two-operation formula as the engine, names both permitted grounds with their printed conditions, and concedes the strongest objection rather than listing the defences.

**Practice question 4 [15]:** "Property is the most potent instrument of depriving others of their freedom." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is the chapter's own, and its force is comparative: **while the few cannot enslave society by pooling their physical and mental powers, they can do so by accumulating unlimited property**, which **can also be used to hire best talents and labour power**.
